[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT16</sty> Similar is the view given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>. “We ought not, for this example, to put implicit confidence in lots, nor to connect with it the instance recorded in the Acts, when Matthias was chosen an Apostle by lot, since privileges granted to individuals cannot make a common rule, (<sty c="000000" i="1">cum privilegia singulorum non possint legem </sty>facere communem.”)
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT17</sty> This answer reverses the order of the questions. He answers the last question first. “Whence comest thou, and what is thy country?” The answer is, “I am Hebrew.” The previous question was, “What is thy work,” or occupation? To this he answers, “I fear Jehovah,” etc. This was his calling, his work, his occupation. “Fear,” of course, includes worship and service. This ought to be the work and occupation of all. But to the first question, as to what evil he had done, he gives no answer. <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> supposes that the whole is not here related, but is to be gathered from what follows. It is, however, probable that he had previously told them, that is, before the storm arose, that he was fleeing from the presence of the Lord: he therefore left them to conclude what evil it was which he had done. It may be inquired why

--- PAGE 130 ---

[BODY] he said that he was “Hebrew,” and not that he was an Israelite, as the case really was, for he belonged to the ten tribes, and not to the kingdom of Judah; and Israelites, and not Hebrews seem to have been then the common name of the ten tribes. The reason may be, that as the Israelites were then for the most part idolatrous, he wished to show that he was a true descendant of the patriarchs, who were God’s faithful servants, real Hebrews, passengers, sojourners, and pilgrims, as the word imparts, on the earth. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT18</sty> “Non Deos quos invocatis, et qui salvare non possunt, sed Deum coeli qui mare fecit et aridam, mare in quo fugio, aridam de qua fugio,” etc. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>.
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT19</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">tyç[ tazAhm</sty>, — “What this thou hast done?” “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">ti> touto</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">epoi>hsav </sty>— what this thou hast done?” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Sept.</sty> “Quid (<sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.</sty>, cur) hoc fecisti — why hast thou done this?” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius;</sty> so <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome,</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius,</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius.</sty> “What is this thou hast done?” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson.</sty> It is not a question, requiring a reason for what he had done, but rather an exclamation — “What an evil is this which thou has done!” They judged of it by the effects; for they knew before that he had fled from the presence of the Lord; and now they perceived how great an evil it was. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT20</sty> Literally, “that the sea may cease from upon us.” The waves were rolling over them; hence <sty c="008080" i="0">wnyl[m</sty>, from upon us. That the sea may be calm <sty c="000000" i="1">around</sty> us, is to give a meaning to the word which it never has. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT21</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">r[mw ˚lwh µyh yk</sty>, “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">o[ti h qalassa eporeueto kai exhgeire</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">mallon kludwna </sty>— for the sea went and more raised the swell.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Sept.</sty> “Quia mare ibat et intumescebat; — for the sea did go and swell.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome, Grotius, </sty>and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate.</sty> “For the sea grew more and more tempestuous.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome.</sty> The verb <sty c="008080" i="0">˚lwh</sty>, connected as here with another verb, does not always mean increase, but continuance. See
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><010803></sty>Genesis 8:3; <sty c="800000" i="0"><170904></sty>Esther 9:4. Literally it is, “For the sea <sty c="000000" i="1">was</sty>
[BODY] going on, and was tempestuous,” that is, it continued to be tempestuous. An increase of violence seemed not to have been hardly possible, for a shipwreck was previously dreaded. <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome,</sty> in a strain rather imaginary, but striking, says, “<sty c="000000" i="1">For the sea went and did swell,</sty> — it <sty c="000000" i="1">went</sty>, as it had been commanded, — it went, to manifest the

--- PAGE 131 ---

[BODY] vengeance of its Lord, — it went, to prosecute a runaway Prophet; and it <sty c="000000" i="1">swelled</sty> at every moment, and rose, as it were, in larger billows, while the mariners were delaying, to show that they could not put off the vengeance of its Creator.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT22</sty> Literally, “and the men labored to return to dry land.” The ideal meaning of <sty c="008080" i="0">rtj</sty> is to dig, or to dig through, <sty c="800000" i="0"><182416></sty>Job 24:16;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><261207></sty>Ezekiel 12:7: but it is here in its secondary sense of laboring or
[BODY] toiling. <sty c="008080" i="0">byçhl</sty>, to return, must be taken here intransitively, though generally it bears in Hiphil a transitive sense. “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Kai> parebiazonto oi</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">andrev tou epistreyai prov thn ghn</sty> — And the men strove to return to land.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Sept.</sty> And the men rowed hard to regain the land.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson. — Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT23</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">hna</sty> and <sty c="008080" i="0">an</sty> are particles of entreaty or exclamation, and may be rendered, “I, or, we pray,” according to the context. Here they should be, “We pray.” They are sometimes rendered, Oh! Alas! Now. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT24</sty> “Hoc est, ne nobis imputes caedem viri justi — Impute not unto us the slaughter of a just man.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius.</sty> See <sty c="800000" i="0"><070924></sty>Judges 9:24;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><402724></sty>Matthew 27:24. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT25</sty> Some render this sentence in the present tense, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, “Tu enim Jehovah sicut vis facis — for thou, Jehovah, doest as thou willest.” The verbs are in the past tense, but this tense in Hebrew includes often both times, — “Thou hast done and doest, as thou hast willed and willest:” and this seems to be the full import of the passage. <sty c="000000" i="1">Mercerius,</sty> quoted by Poole, gives this paraphrase, — “All these things have taken place through thine appointment, — that Jonah came to the ship, that a storm has been raised, that the lot has fallen on Jonah, and that he has confessed his sin: we unwillingly do this dreadful deed, but this is understood to be thy will.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> took the words as referring to the time then present, for he expresses the meaning thus: “Tu vis ut in mare dejeciatur: fiat igitur quod vis; nam voluntati tuae quis resistat? Thou willest that he should be cast into the sea: be then that done which thou willest; for who can resist thy will?” According to this view, it is an expression of acquiescence in God’s will respecting Jonah. But both <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> retain our common version. <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> reads, “Tu enim, O Jova, pro arbitratu tuo agis. — For thou, O Jehovah, doest according to thine own will.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 132 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT26</sty> “The first,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Adam Clarke,</sty> “that perhaps was ever offered on board a vessel, since the ark floated on the waters of the great deluge.” How uselessly have learned men often employed their talents, trying to improve what is simply and plainly related. <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome </sty>allegorizes this act of the mariners, and says that their sacrifice was that of praise. A Jewish Rabbi, <sty c="000000" i="1">Jonathan,</sty> makes an addition to the sentence — that they <sty c="000000" i="1">said</sty> that they would sacrifice to Jehovah; and <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> adopts his addition. Another Rabbi, <sty c="000000" i="1">Elieser,</sty> has fabricated a fine tale, that they returned immediately to Joppa, went up to Jerusalem, and were circumcised! The fact alone is stated here, as to when and where we know nothing, and it signifies nothing to us; only that the most obvious import of the narrative is, that they <sty c="000000" i="1">did</sty> offer a sacrifice on board the vessel, immediately after the storm ceased. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT27</sty>. The verb <sty c="008080" i="0">hnm</sty> does not necessarily include the idea of creation, but its meaning is, to distribute, to arrange, to order, to provide, to prepare; and yet this preparation may involve the exercise of a creative power, as in the case of the gourd mentioned in the fourth chapter, for it is the same verb. Though it might be an indigenous plant, yet to provide it so quickly in one night was the act of Divine power. So also as to this fish, it might or it might not have been one usually found in that part of the sea. To provide it, but bringing it to the spot, was as much a miracle as to provide it by a new act of creation. To allow the one and to deny the other, is wholly unreasonable. The whole was clearly miraculous: and the discovery of any such fish now does not in the least render the transaction less miraculous. Every part of it must be ascribed to a Divine interposition; and let those stumble who are resolved to stumble. It has been supposed to have been the “whale,” because the word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">khtov</sty> is used in the reference made to Jonah in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><401240></sty>Matthew 12:40: but that word is a general term applied to a
[BODY] large fish, and does not necessarily mean the whale. <sty c="0000d4" i="0">khtov</sty>, says <sty c="000000" i="1">Ribera</sty>, in his comment on Jonah, non certae speciei, sed generis nomen est, et piscem quemvis magnum significat. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER TWO.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT28</sty> It is remarkable that several sentences in this prayer, as it has been observed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Marchius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson, </sty>are exactly the same, not only

--- PAGE 133 ---

[BODY] in sense, but also, in most instances, in words, with passages in the Psalms. The first clause of this verse is found in <sty c="800000" i="0"><19C001></sty>Psalm 120:1, only the words are differently arranged. The last clause of the third verse, both in words and order, is the same with a distich in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><194207></sty>Psalm 42:7. The beginning of the fourth verse agrees nearly with <sty c="800000" i="0"><193122></sty>Psalm 31:22; and so does the fifth verse with a line in <sty c="800000" i="0"><196901></sty>Psalm 69:1, one word being different. The first clause of the
[BODY] seventh verse is found in the very same words in <sty c="800000" i="0"><19E203></sty>Psalm 142:3; and the first line in the eighth verse is <sty c="800000" i="0"><193106></sty>Psalm 31:6, with the exception of one letter; and the last words of the ninth verse are to be met within <sty c="800000" i="0"><190308></sty>Psalm 3:8, only the order is reversed. “On the supposition,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “that Jonah was familiar with the Psalms, it was very natural for him to incorporate sentences taken from them with his own language.”<sty c="000000" i="1"> — Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT29</sty> “No place amiss for prayer, <sty c="000000" i="1">I will that men pray everywhere;</sty> where ever God casts us we may find a way open heavenwards, if it be now our own fault. Jonah was now in the bottom of the sea, yet out of the depths he cries to God.”<sty c="000000" i="1"> — M. Henry.</sty> “It may be asked, How could Jonah either pray or breathe in the stomach of a fish? Very easily, if God so willed it. And let the reader keep this constantly in view: the whole is a <sty c="000000" i="1">miracle</sty>, from Jonah’s being swallowed by the fish, till he was cast ashore by the same animal. It was God that had <sty c="000000" i="1">prepared</sty> the great fish; it was the Lord that <sty c="000000" i="1">spake</sty> to the fish, and <sty c="000000" i="1">caused</sty> it to vomit Jonah on the dry land. All is miracle.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Adam Clarke. — Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT30</sty> He relates here, as it appears from the preceding, “and he said,” the prayer he offered when in the fish’s bowels, and not a prayer offered after his deliverance. Some have entertained the latter opinion, because some of the verbs here are in the past tense: but this circumstance only shows that he continued to pray from the time when he was swallowed by the fish to the time when he was delivered. It was a continued act. It is the same as though he said, “I have called, and do call on Jehovah.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius,</sty> and also <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius,</sty> render the verbs in the present tense, “I call,” etc. The following is a translation according to the view of this prayer, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">3. I call in my distress on Jehovah, and he will answer me;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">From the belly of the grave I cry, — thou hearest my voice.</sty>

--- PAGE 134 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">4. When thou didst send me to the deep, into the midst of the waters,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the flood surrounded me, —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Thy billows and waves over me passed;</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">5. Then I said, I am banished from the sight of thine eyes; —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yet I will again look towards the temple of thy holiness.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">6. Encompass me do the waters to the soul,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The deep surrounds me,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The sedge is wrapped around my head:</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">7. To the cuttings off of the mountains have I descended;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The earth! Its bars</sty><sty c="000080" i="0"> are</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> continually around me:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">But thou wilt bring from destruction my life,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">O Jehovah, my God.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">8. When overwhelmed within me was my soul,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Jehovah did I remember;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And come to thee shall my prayer —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To the temple of thy holiness.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">9. They who regard idols of vanity,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Their own mercy forsake:</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">10. But I, with the voice of praise, will sacrifice to thee,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">What I have vowed will I fulfill:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Salvation </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">belongs</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> to Jehovah.</sty>

[BODY] “The cuttings off,” in verse 7, says <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst,</sty> were those parts which were <sty c="000000" i="1">cut off</sty> from the mountains at the deluge. The Septuagint has <sty c="0000d4" i="0">scismav</sty><sty c="008080" i="0"> </sty>— rents-clefts. Roots, bottoms, foundations, have been adopted by some, but not consistently with the meaning of the original word, — “The bars or bolts” of the earth convey the idea of impediments in his way to return to the earth. They were “around” him, or literally “upon” him, <sty c="008080" i="0">yd[b</sty>, that is, they were, as it were, closed upon him. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT31</sty> “He calls them God’s billows and his waves, not because he made and rules them, but because he had now commissioned them against Jonah, and ordered them to afflict and terrify, but not to destroy him. These words are plainly quoted by Jonah from <sty c="800000" i="0"><194207></sty>Psalm 42:7. What David spoke figuratively and metaphorically, Jonah applies to himself as literally fulfilled.”<sty c="000000" i="1"> — M. Henry.</sty>

--- PAGE 135 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT32</sty> “Here prayer is <sty c="000000" i="1">personified,</sty> and is represented as a <sty c="000000" i="1">messenger</sty> going from the <sty c="000000" i="1">distressed</sty> and entering into the temple of God, and standing before him. This is a very fine and delicate image.”<sty c="000000" i="1"> — Adam Clarke.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT33</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">awçAylbh</sty>, “Idols of vanity or falsehood,” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.,</sty> false, or deceitful, or vain idols. <sty c="008080" i="0">lbh</sty> means vapor, smoke, breath, vanity, inanity: but in the plural number it is applied for the most part to idols. See
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><053221></sty>Deuteronomy 32:21; <sty c="800000" i="0"><111613></sty>1 Kings 16:13, 26. <sty c="008080" i="0">awç</sty> is a lie,
[BODY] which is vain — useless, and false — deceptive. <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> renders the words, “Vanitates inanitatis — vanities of inanity’” <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius,</sty> “Vanitates mendaces — mendacious vanities;” <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint,</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">mataia kai Yeudh</sty><sty c="008080" i="0"> </sty>— vain and false things.” “He thus calls idols,” says an author in Poole’s Syn., “and all those things in which any one, excluding God, trusts; which are nothing, and can do nothing, and which deceive their worshippers.” This is true, that is, that all other things, as well as idols, are, apart from God, vain, and worthless, and deceptive; but the reference here no doubt is to idols. They are not only empty, but deceptive. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT34</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">wbz[y µdsj</sty>, “Their mercy or goodness they forsake,” that is, the mercy exhibited and offered to them by God; or, if we render it goodness, it means their chief good, which is God. The Psalmist calls God his goodness in <sty c="800000" i="0"><19E402></sty>Psalm 144:2, <sty c="008080" i="0">ydsj</sty>, “my goodness,” the giver of all his goodness, or his chief good. <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> gives very correctly the meaning of the two lines in these words —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“Qui vana idola colunt,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Felicitatis suae auctorem deserunt —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">They who worship vain idols,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Desert the author of their own happiness.”</sty>

[INDENT] More literally —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“They who attend on the idols of vanity,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Their own goodness forsake.”</sty>

[BODY] There is a contrast between vain idols and their own goodness, that is, the goodness received by them from God. <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> gives this paraphrase, “They who worship idols are vain; for they forsake their own mercy, that is, God, who is able to help them in their distress.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry</sty> suggests another view, “They who follow their own inventions, as Jonah had done, when he fled from the presence of the lord to go to

--- PAGE 136 ---

[BODY] Tarshish, forsake their own mercy, that mercy which they may find in God.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 3</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT35</sty> Literally, “And proclaim to or against her the proclamation which I declare to thee.” The Septuagint is, “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Kai khruxon en auth kata to</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">khrugma to emprosqen o{ egw elalhsa prov se </sty>— And preach in it the former preaching which I have spoken to thee.” <sty c="008080" i="0">Hyl[</sty> in five MSS., as in chapter 1:2, “against her,” and not <sty c="008080" i="0">hyla</sty>, “to her.” <sty c="008080" i="0">dyla</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">rbd ykna rça</sty>, “which I <sty c="000000" i="1">am</sty> speaking or declaring to thee.” <sty c="008080" i="0">rbd</sty> is a participle; being preceded by a nominative, it will admit of an auxiliary verb either in the past, present or future tense, according to the context; though it is often used to express the present time.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders the sentence thus — “And cry unto her <sty c="000000" i="1">in</sty> the words which I <sty c="000000" i="1">shall </sty>speak unto thee;” <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> more paraphrastically thus — “And make the proclamation to it which I order thee;” and adds the following remarks, — “Be my herald, and faithfully deliver my message. The word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">khrux</sty> in Greek answers to the Hebrew <sty c="008080" i="0">arwq</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">kore</sty>, both signifying a <sty c="000000" i="1">crier,</sty> a <sty c="000000" i="1">herald,</sty> a <sty c="000000" i="1">preacher;</sty> one that makes proclamation with a <sty c="000000" i="1">loud</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">earnest </sty>cry. Such was John Baptist,
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><234003></sty>Isaiah 40:3; such was Jesus Christ, <sty c="800000" i="0"><430718></sty>John 7:18-37; and
[BODY] such were all his apostles. And such earnestness becomes a ministry that has to do with immortal souls, asleep and dead in sin, hanging on the brink of perdition, and insensible of their state. The soft speaking, gentle toned, unmoved preacher, is never likely to awaken souls.”
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry</sty> considers that the commission was not specifically explained to him then. “Jonah must go,” he says, “with implicit faith: he shall not know till he comes thither what message he must deliver.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT36</sty> The original is, “And Nineveh was a city great to God” —
[BODY] <sty c="008080" i="0">hlwdgAry[ µyhlal</sty>. The remark of <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry</sty> is, “So the Hebrew phrase is, meaning no more than as we render it, <sty c="000000" i="1">exceeding great;</sty> this honor that language doth to the great God, that great things derive their denomination from him” Though the form of the expression here is different from what we find in other places, when God is taken in this sense, as in <sty c="800000" i="0"><198010></sty>Psalm 80:10, <sty c="008080" i="0">laAyzra</sty>, cedars of God, that is, tall

--- PAGE 137 ---

[BODY] or great cedars, — yet there is no other sense that comports with this place. This is the view of <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius, Drusius, Newcome,</sty> and many others. Some render it, <sty c="000000" i="1">great through God,</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> seems to have taken it in this sense, for he explains it by “Deo eam augente — God having increased it.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> considers <sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty> here in the sense of <sty c="008080" i="0">ygpl</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">before,</sty> and refers to <sty c="800000" i="0"><011009></sty>Genesis 10:9. [BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT37</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">µyhlab wnymayw</sty>, “And they believed in God. The verb <sty c="008080" i="0">ˆma</sty> in Hiphil is ever followed by <sty c="008080" i="0">b</sty> or <sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>, except in one instance by <sty c="008080" i="0">ta</sty> in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><071120></sty>Judges 11:20. When followed by <sty c="008080" i="0">b</sty> it seems to mean, to give
[BODY] credit to what is said, to believe one’s testimony, or the truth of what is referred to. To believe then in God is to believe the truth of what he declares, to believe his word. Hence in <sty c="800000" i="0"><142020></sty>2 Chronicles 20:20, Jehosophat said to the people, “Believe in the Lord your God,” <sty c="008080" i="0">µkyhla hwhyb wnymah</sty>; and he adds, “Believe [in] his Prophets,” <sty c="008080" i="0">wyaybnb wnymah</sty>. It is the word of God, and the word of the Prophets, which was the same, or the truth or veracity of God and of his Prophets, that they For I have believed [in] thy commandments,” <sty c="008080" i="0">˚ytwxmb</sty>, that is, in the truth of thy commandments. — When the verb in Hiphil is followed by <sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>, the idea of reliance or dependance is more especially conveyed, though in many instances there is hardly a difference tobe recognized, except the context be minutely observed.
[BODY] Among other passages, the verb in its Hiphil form is followed by <sty c="008080" i="0">b</sty>, in

[BODY] The Septuagint renders believing <sty c="000000" i="1">in</sty> God by <sty c="0000d4" i="0">epi>steusan tw qew</sty>: so does Paul in <sty c="800000" i="0"><450403></sty>Romans 4:3, <sty c="800000" i="0"><480306></sty>Galatians 3:6; but he retains the Hebrew form in <sty c="800000" i="0"><450405></sty>Romans 4:5, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">pisteuonti epi ton</sty>, etc. <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin </sty>here conveys the same meaning by “crediderunt Deo — believed God:” that is, the Ninevites gave credit to what God declared by Jonah, they believed God’s word. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT38</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius, </sty>as well as <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius,</sty> had the same view of the verse, by rendering the verb in the tense here proposed. <sty c="000000" i="1">Quia</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">pervenerat</sty> is the version of the former; and the version of the latter is, <sty c="000000" i="1">Quu enim pervenisset.</sty> Our own version and that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> seem also to favor this view, by rendering <sty c="008080" i="0">w</sty> “for,” as giving a reason for what is said in the preceding verse: but <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> has “and,” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> the same, and also the Septuagint. [BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT39</sty> Who this king was is a matter of conjecture. “About thirteen years,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome,</sty> “after the death of Jeroboam II., king of Israel, Pul, king of Assyria, invaded Israel. So that Pul, or his predecessor, may have been the king here mentioned.” Others think that he was Sardanapalus, a character notorious in history for his luxurious, effeminate, and debauched life. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT40</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">On th nhsteia prosescen</sty>, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">alla th apoch twn kakwn</sty> — “He [God] did not regard fasting, but abstinence from evils.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>. “It is not enough,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry,</sty> “to fast for sin, but we must fast from sin, and in order to the success of our prayers, must no more regard iniquity in our hearts… The work of a fast-day is not done with the day; no, then the hardest and most needful part of the work begins, which is to turn from sin, and to live a new life, and not to return with the dog to his vomit.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT41</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> has omitted to notice the words at the beginning of the seventh verse. [BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT42</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">jqzjb</sty>, with vigor. “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Ektevw~v </sty>— intensely, earnestly.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Sept.</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Vehementer — </sty>vehemently.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius. </sty>“<sty c="000000" i="1">Totis viribus — </sty>with all their powers.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Mercerius.</sty> “<sty c="000000" i="1">Cum intensione valida</sty> — with strong intensity” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius.</sty> “In prayer,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry,</sty> “we must cry

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT44</sty> A <sty c="000000" i="1">rapacite, </sty>from robbery, extortion, plunder <sty c="008080" i="0">smjhAzm</sty>, from violence, outrage, or injustice don by force or violence: it means tyrannical injustice. “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Apo thv adikiav</sty> — from injustice,” wrong, iniquity. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Sept. </sty>But as it is said to be in their hand, it means, by a metonymy, the plunder got by injustice, exercised tyrannically.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius </sty>observes that the similitude here is first taken from the feet, and then taken from the hands. [CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER FOUR</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT45</sty> The two verbs used here are evidently to be construed as impersonals. The most literal rendering would be thus, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“And it was an evil to Jonah, a great evil;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">and wrath was to him,” or, “he was wroth.”</sty>

[BODY] Evil means often grief or distress, and so it is here: but the verb <sty c="008080" i="0">hrj</sty>, properly rendered in our version, “very angry,” seldom if ever, means grief. It is sometimes rendered “grieved” in our version; but in every instance that I can find, it means the grief of anger or displeasure. It occurs twice in Genesis 4, in exactly similar form as we find it here, followed by the preposition <sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>, <sty c="008080" i="0">dam ˆyql rjyw</sty>, “and Cain was very wroth,” or literally, “and there was wrath to Cain very much,” version

--- PAGE 140 ---

[INDENT] 5. And then in the following verse we have the like form, <sty c="008080" i="0">dl hrj</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">hml</sty>, “Why are thou wroth?” or, “Why is wrath to thee?” The phrase here is, <sty c="008080" i="0">rjyw wl</sty>, “and wrath was to him.” What seems to have made some commentators to change wrath or anger to grief, as has been a desire to screen the guilt of Jonah. But the whole narrative clearly shows that he was so displeased as to be angry. His pettish request to die is a sufficient proof of this. Calvin was not a man to trifle with the word of God for this purpose, or for any other: nor was he at a loss to account for the sin of Jonah, without denying his piety. It is only shallow Christians, and such as have only the outward shell of religion, who are reduced to this dilemma.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> very justly observes, “That though all the works of God are altogether blameless (irreprehensibilia,) yet there is hardly anything which is not sometimes blamed by our most foolish flesh (carne insipientissima.) Thus not only the world, God’s enemy, sins against him; but even his own people, who honor him, openly show, one while, that his severity, and at another time that his kindness, displease them, as though they were not befitting.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT46</sty> Added here shall be <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius’</sty> excellent explanation of this passate, as given in <sty c="800000" i="0"><290213></sty>Joel 2:13. It corresponds materially with that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin,</sty> — “Ipse est <sty c="008080" i="0">ˆwnj</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">gratiosus, </sty>apud LXX., et Hieronymum,<sty c="000000" i="1"> </sty><sty c="0000d4" i="0">elehmwn</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">benignus</sty>, h. e. ut voces junctas sic distinguamus commodé, in creaturas praeter omne earum meritum <sty c="000000" i="1">benefictus;</sty> deinde <sty c="008080" i="0">µwjr</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">misericors,</sty> apud LXX., <sty c="0000d4" i="0">oikti>rmwn</sty>, h.e. tenerrimo amore motus in miseros ad illus succurrendum et indulgendum; tum <sty c="008080" i="0">µypa ˚ra</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">longanumis, </sty>apud LXX., et Hieronymum, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">makroqumov</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">patiens,</sty> h.e. tardus ad iram, seu judicia sua satis facilé et satis diu differens; adhuc <sty c="008080" i="0">dsjAbr</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">amplus</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">benignitate,</sty> apud LXX., et hieronymum, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">polueleov</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">multae</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">misericordiae,</sty> h.e. bonitatem demonstrans intensissime, latissime et diutissime, inter homines; tandem quod aliis vocibus in Exodo exprimitur, <sty c="008080" i="0">t[rh l[ µjn</sty>, non <sty c="000000" i="1">consolabilis,</sty> sed <sty c="000000" i="1">poenitens super</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">malo,</sty> apud LXX., <sty c="0000d4" i="0">metanown epi< taiv kakiaiv</sty>, apud Hieronymum, <sty c="000000" i="1">praestabilis super malitia, </sty>sive <sty c="000000" i="1">poenitens, — </sty>h.e. malum non culpae ab hominibus commissum, sed poenae, seu afflictionis, juxta Hieronymum, coll. <sty c="800000" i="0"><400634></sty>Matthew 6:34, à se immittendum aut immissum homini, ad hujus resipiscentiam facilé arcens vel auferens,

[BODY] adeoque <sty c="000000" i="1">placabilis,</sty> juxta propositum suum immutabile et promissa generalia, <sty c="800000" i="0"><241807></sty>Jeremiah 18:7, 8; 26:3, etc., et finem judicii omnis, qui est, malum peccati dedocere. [CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT47</sty> This clause has been variously rendered. The original words are these,
[BODY] <sty c="008080" i="0">˚l hrj bfyhh</sty>. It is not to the point to say, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Dr. Henderson</sty> does, that the first word is sometimes rendered adverbially, as meaning “greatly,” etc.; for in other places it is rendered as a verb, “to be good,” or “to do good.” It depends wholly on the form of the sentence what rendering is the most suitable. Both the construction of the words and the connection of the passage seem to favor the literal rendering. The first <sty c="008080" i="0">h</sty> is the <sty c="000000" i="1">an</sty> of the Latin, <sty c="000000" i="1">whether,</sty> it is an interrogation. Then the rendering is, “Whether it is good,” or, “Is that good?” Jonah had said before that it was “good” for him to die rather than to live; for it is the same word in a different form — <sty c="008080" i="0">yyjm ytwm bwf yk</sty>, “for good my death rather than my life.” Then the question to him is, “Is it good <sty c="000000" i="1">that</sty> thou art wrathful,” or literally, “<sty c="000000" i="1">that</sty> wrath is to thee?” the verb <sty c="008080" i="0">hrj</sty>, as in the first verse, is to be construed impersonally.
[BODY] The rendering, “Art thou much vexed?” is pointless and vapid. It is indeed countenanced by the Septuagint; but we must remember that on some points there is a wrong leaning in that version, and this is one of those points. As in the case of Jerome and the early Fathers, there was a disposition and an attempt to lessen and even to obliterate all the faults and defects of the ancient saints, so it was evidently with the authors of this version. Superstition and Pharisaism must have all their saints perfect, while the word of God represents all true saints as imperfect in the present state, but aiming at, and longing for, perfection, and fully expecting to enjoy it hereafter.
[BODY] The version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> is, “Doest thou well that <sty c="000000" i="1">thine anger</sty> is kindled?” Kindling or heat, that is, of anger or wrath, is the ideal

--- PAGE 142 ---

[BODY] meaning of the verb. <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius,</sty> as well as <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius,</sty> consider that anger, and not grief is meant. <sty c="000000" i="1">Dr. Adam Clarke’s</sty> version is this, “Is anger good for thee?” which is certainly very literal; or, as the verb is in the causative mood, it might be rendered, “Can anger do good to thee?” It may be doubted whether here, and in the ninth verse, where the same phrase twice occurs, this rendering is the most obvious and natural. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT48</sty> Appended here is this note in the margin, — “Putavit, cessante horologio, se ante tempus finire;” — “He thought that, through the clock stopping, he had finished before the time.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT49</sty> Much has been written on the character of this plant. Modern critics have pretended to determine that it was the <sty c="000000" i="1">Ricinus,</sty> commonly called <sty c="000000" i="1">Palma Christi</sty>. It matters not what it was: its growth was doubtless miraculous. It may have been an indigenous plant, it may have been such a plant as never grew before or after. Two things are evident — God prepared it, and prepared it to shelter Jonah. In a translation it would have been better either to retain the original name, or to give it the general name of a plant or shrub. To call it a gourd, an ivy, or a cucumber, is to convey an incorrect idea. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0"> FT50</sty> It is literally, “the son of a night,” but as <sty c="000000" i="1">cucurbita,</sty> gourd, is feminine, <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> adopted <sty c="000000" i="1">filia</sty> instead of <sty c="000000" i="1">filius. — Ed.</sty>
